PARTICIPATIONBYTHE UNITEDSTATES GEOGRAPHIC BOARD
Itis the function of the United States Geographic Boardtoestablish uni-
form usage in regard to geographic nomenclature and orthography throughout
the executive departments of the Government, and particularly upon the maps
and charts issued bythevariousdepartments and bureaus..Allunsettled
questions concerning geographic names which arise in the departments are to
bereferred to the board and its decisions to be accepted by themasthe stand-
ard authority in such matters.The board hastheadditional duty of deter-
mining, changing, and Axing place names within the United States and insular
possessions, and all names suggested for any place by anyofficeroremployee
of the Government are required to be referred to said boardforit6 approval
before publication.
Since the boarddoes notattempt the impossible task
ofpassingonthe
myriadsofnames published by other competent authority or the vasternum-
ber in localuse,the fieldforgazetteers of particular regionsisleft unoccupied.
The late secretaryofthe board and others urged the publicationofour entire
collection of Virgin Islands names, to serveasastandardofreference, the
nucleusofafuture geographic encyclopedia of this region.
NOfunds for publication were available, however, until March,1924,when
theMS.was rapidly typed, and two weeks later, April2,1924,exhibitedat
the monthly meetingofthe board, withacordial invitationforconstrpctive
criticism;marked sheets, later replaced bycards,being flled for that purpose
with the secretary.
Allnames herein, whetherof major orminorimportance,nototherwise
sufficiently established,e.g.,byprior publicationorlocaluse,have been referred
to the UnitedStates Geographic hoard for action, accompanied with ample
data; and decisions were obtained on all dubious cases of any moment.The
just-lasued“IndextotheFifthReportandSupplement(1830-23)ofthe
United States Geographic Board,1924,”lists225Barnes, adoptedorrejected
agreeably todataand recommendations prepared by the Coast and Geodetic
Survey, for entryinthe present work.Pursuant toamotion adopted April
2.1924,aspecial meeting was culled in June, to considerallremaining cases
affectingthiswork, and requiring decision.
A certain number of relatively unimportant“last-moment”queMions, such
asassigning the nameofamilltoitshill, an estate to its bay, etc., being
obviouely impliedinany case, were canceled,inthe absenceoffurtheraction
bythe board during the few weeks remainingerethe closeofthe fiscalyear.
However, every established name discoverableasthe resultofexhaustivere-
March willbefound herein, with citationsofcompetent authorities.

I‘The possessive form shouldbeavoided, wheneveritcanbe done without

The nominative singular should be accorded preference.
Citing possessiveor

“Discontinuetheuse of hyphensinconnecting parts of names”and“sim-

Theidealin geo-
graphical nomenclatureisnsingle name forasingle feature.Whenthe
elements ofacompound name are monosyllabic,theabove ruleisapplied
with ease,In this region the names generally are found to havebeenalready
consolidatedinto one word,bythe peculiarityofthe Danish idiom, which
favors eventhejoining of the specific proper name with,its “genericappel-
lative”(featuralterm),these,however,beingregularlyseparatedbythe
English idiom.Hence, decompounding a t timesisnot only requisite, but may
preserveauseful distinction.E.g.,English‘‘Cane Bay,” Danish“Canebay,”

yields much to custom.
“ I tisdesirable to avoid the use of diacritic characters.”
Religiously ob-
served, in Englishoranglicized names; but, in those retaining their foreign
identity, mutilation obscures both the name anditsmeaning.Danish“ 0 ”
and‘‘8’’nre distinct letters ofthe alphabet, with different sounds,asare
the Spanish‘‘N”und“R.”
“Geographic names,likepersonal names,shouldnot be translated.”Ia-
fractions have been frequent and lamentable, producingamonotonous Iteration
ofcommonplace names, whereas their retention intheoriginal language used
by discoverers or residents would have preservedatonce historic associations
and even more useful geographic distinctions.Only when thereisinvolveda
slightchange ofform, withnonein meuning, woulditbepedantic notto
usethevernacular.E.g.,“Lemon”(not“Limon”),“Whitesand”(not
“Hviide Sand”).
FORMAND ARRANGEMENTOFTHE NOTICES
I n recordingthe names in this work certain minordepartures have been
madefrompreviouscustom.Whenthepropernamecannotretainits
specific application ifdeprivedofitsgeneric term,thislastisconstrued to
be an inseparable part of the name, which,therefore,isgiveninfull, just
asitwould appearonthe map.E.g.,“St.Thomas Harbor;”notI‘St.Thomas;
Harbor;”I‘Water Island;”not“Water;Island.”Onthe other hand, when
the name can stand alone, with Its generic term impliedorunderstood, the
latter is relegated to be the flrst wordinthe descrlptlon.E.g.,“St.Croix;
Island;”‘‘Maho;Quarter;”“Mafolle;Estate.”
The abundance of alternative namesandvariant spellingsina polyglot com-
munityisdisconcertlng;and,inthe descriptions, association must guide the
choiceofnames tobementioned.Specialeffortwasmadetoclear upcon-

TheestatesonSt. Thomas andSt.John were nearlyallestablished during

T E R M SREFERRINGT OT H EE S T A T E S
Theword“plantage”occurs constantly on early mapsfnseveral languages,
in histories, and in the public judicial records,asaportionofthenameofthe
originalestates.E.g.,‘ IJohndeWindt‘sPlantage”Interwasknownas
BethlehemEstate.”“Plantage”isusedregularlywiththenameofthe
original patentee, proprietor,orplanter.After some hesitation,itseemed well
to leaveitunchanged, when thus used.
Plantagegrund”(plural“Plantagegrunde”)was andisthe offlcial term
appliedtotheestate tracts, hereinusuallyreferredtosimplyas‘‘tracts”
orby number. They were designated by serial numbers, beginningwith“1,”
and running consecutively( I ‘boustrophedon,”back and forth) through each of
t h e quarters.Each tract measured2,000by3,000Danish feet(equivalent to
2,059.378by3,089.067English feet) i n extent;or,inarea,150Danish acres,
each100alen ,square,covering40,000Danish squarefeet,and equivalent to
slightly more than146.04English acres, each covering43,500Englishsquare
See note under“St.Croix.”
feet.
The‘‘Plantegegrunde”(estate tracts)were disposedofinunits, multiples,
o r submultiples, to colonists, whow family names in many instances a r e still
associated with the present estates.The considerationforthe groundineach
casewas merely a n annual rentalofone turkey, deliverable to the governor
forhisannual public banquet.
The English term‘Iplantation”never gained vogue.The early‘‘plantages”
came to be known insteadas“estates,” which termisnow used exclusively.

longingto the plantation:second, the“ma~ise”orplantationhouse, inSt.

I‘estate-village.”

which eachoftheforegoing terms was employed.

DEFINITIONSANDDESCRIPTIONS
Asystematic scheme was adopted forthearrangement and wording ofthe
definitionsofthevariousclassesofnames.Thedesignw a s tostatethe

hensivedescriptionwiththeutmostconcisenesscompatible withclearness,

to inscribe the name in its appropriate place, with full assumnce.Acknowl-
edgmentismade for valuable suggestions contained in several papersinthe
"Atti"ofthe International Congress of Geography, held a t Rome in1013.

and Geodetic Survey.Geographic positions, from the same source, being fresh
and available informationofpotential value to surveyors and geographers,me
noted for absolute location.'"Vorthe convenience of m:iriners.the widthsof

ThevegetationoftheVirginIslandsisnaturallydivisibleintosix

Manyoftheislandsarerocky o r sandyandbarren.Lessthanathird of
thegroup i s under cultivation.Theproducts include sugar, molasses, rum,
coffee,maize, vegetables, fruits, ginger, pimento,turmeric, indigo, cotton,

ingexcellent pasturage.Forests, though few, contain mahogany,nut,and
otherusefultrees.’The coast waters teem with numerous speciesoffood
fish.Total population, about40,000.
The climateiswarm, equable, agreeable, healthful, characterized by two
wetand two dry seasons.The North Atlantic trade wind blows steadily
from north-northeast for three-fourthsofthe year,the breeze becoming
brisk about4o’clock each morning and afternoon.Onchundred and thirty
destructive hurricanesarerecorded, with wind velocityashighas150miles
The islands lie directly in the mean trackofhurricanes.These
a n hour.
occur most frequently betweenAugustand October.Earth tremorsare
frequent, but never severe.
The group was discovered by Columbus onhissecond voyage.November
14, 1493,h e anchoredatthe entrance ofthe bayjustwestofSaltriver
Point, 8t. Croix, to obtain fresh water, and hadaskirmish with the natives,
who called the island Agay.
The long lineofbold hilltops northward,astheyemerged overthehorizon,
would conveytheimpression of innumerable little islands, while“the long
processionofwhite, surf-beaten fringing reefs appearstohave suggested
afanciful resemblancetothe11,OOOBritish maidens oftheSt.Ursula
legend.”HencetheSpanishname“LasIslasdelasVlrgenes,” or simply
I‘LasVlergenes”;French,“LesVierges”or“liesdes Vierges”;Danish,
Jomfruprerne’’;German,‘‘Jungfern-Inseln.”Thenuniesofthethree
larger islands were bestowed by Columbus himself.The smaller cuye were
named bythebuccaneers.
Theview0 4theislands from shipboardismost picturesque.The sky
line,brokenbymultitudinousdrygulliesandlittlecoves,reminded
Kingsleyofthe Auvergne Mountains:”Their water linehasbeen exposed
t o the gnawingofthe seaa tthepresent level,andevcrywliercthecliffs
arefreshly broken, toppIing down in dust and bowlders, and leaving de-
tachedstacks and skerries.Mostbeautiful meanwhilearethewinding
channelsofblue water, like landlocked lakes, which p a r t the Virgins from
each other;a n d beautiful the white triangular sailsofthe canoe-rigged
craft, which bent up and down them through strong currents and cockling
seas.Theclearair,thestill, soft outline, the rich yet delicate coloring,

foraffjncent cays, makiogatotalof$2.24squaremiles.The islandis

St. Croixisseparated from St. Thomas byadeep of1,100to2,450
fathoma. Itrises steepto600to800fathoms fromseaon northwest and
west.Edgeofsubmarine shelf, equal to island in area, is marked by10

Shoals along entire south coastfor2or3miles oflshore.Climate warm

rainy season, August to December;average precipitation,47.56inches.
The historyisvaried and evatful.St. Croix has belonged successively
totheCaribs,Spanish, English,French,Dutch, Danes, andAmericans.
The island was discovered by Columbus on his aecmd voyage, November14,
1493.H eanchored,iti6reported,near Saltriver Point to obtain water,
and met Indians stmilar to the Carib1of(fuadeloupe, with Arawak cap-
tives from Porto Rico.The natives called the island, AyaiorAgay.
Here
wasfoughtthedrat pitched battleinAmericabetweenthe red natives and
thewhite invaders.Thesavage Caribsofb o t hsexes desperately attacked
the armorcladSpaniards, killing one withapoisoned arrow.
Later, the
slaveraiders from Hispanlola seized mRnynatives,andaproclamation by
CharlesVordered their extermination.
In60years they had vanished.
TheEarlofCumberlandreportedSt. Croixawilderness.Twocolo-
nies,oneofDutch,the otherofFrenchsettlers, arrivedin1625and
were joined by Frenchrefugees.In1646the populationwas over800.
Afterabloody clash with the English, in1649,the Dutch retired to St.
Eustatius and St. Martin.In1660,1,200Spaniards from Porto Ricointurn
massacred the English,t h eremnantescaping to St. Kitts and Bermuda.
The French governor,DePoincy, in1651,sent De Vaugalan with160men
fromet.Rittstoevict the Spaniards.To clear out the fatal miasma, the
French flred the dense forests, retreated aboarcl their ships, to observe the
va5tconflkgration,lastingseveralmonths,thenreturned,plantedthe
ground, and prosp&edgreatly.The soil was amazingly fertile, yielding
abundant cropsoftobacco, cotton, anatto, indigo, andsugar.Within11
years the white population numbered822.‘

BIOdRAPHICALSKETCHOF ADMIRAL PILLSBrfRY
j o h n Elliott Pillsbury was bornatLowell,Mass.,on December15,1846;
thesonofJohn Gilman and Elizabeth Wlmble Pillsbury.Hegraduatedfrom
theUnitedStatesNaval Academyin1867;waspromotedtotherankof
lilnslgnIn1868,masterIn1870,and lieutenantin1872.h1873hemarried
Miss Florence Greenwood Aitchison,ofPortland, Me.Hew a s exceptionally
devoted tohishome and family.
Lieutenant Pillsburyservedonvarious duties andstations, including one
year in the Hydrographic OfflceoftheNavy and ten years IntheU.8.Coast
Survey.I nthisbureau, flrstashydrographic inspector,hesummarizedthe
resultsofdeep-seasoundings obtainedbytheSteamerBlakeinlsSO-lSS3~
to determine theconfigurationofthewest Atlantic Oceanbed;andlaterhewag
Pesponsibleforthefinal verificationandrevisionofimportant portionsof
t h e Atlantic CoastPilot.
TheplansformulatedbyProfessorBache,Superintendent oftheU.5.
Coast Survey between1844and1866,providing forasystematic investlgation
oftheGulfbtrepm,recelvedagreat impetus towardtheir complete accom-
plishment, when, in1884,SuperintendentHilgardassigned LieutenantPills-
bury tothecommandofthesurvey steamer
Blake,which,fortheensuing
seven years, was engaged14the investigationoftheGulfStreamcurrents from
their sourceIntheCaribbean along their course to beyondtheNewfoundland
BankaBy a n original devlce,Pillsbury anchoredtheBlakeinthestream
itself, over depthsoffrom%to2%miles, and secured observationsatvarious
depths belowthesurface,employing ingenious instruments and apparatusof
hlsowninvention.Hedeflnitely locatedthea?&ofthe streamintheFlorida
StraitsandofpCape Hatteras,besidesascertaining many important factsper-
tainingtoitsoriginandcauses,temperatureandsalinity,directionand
loclty and position, and thetidale f f winitofthe sun and
moon.Hiscontributions t oaknowledgeoftheimmutable laws controllingits
movementsbroughtwithlntherangeofscientificpredictionthepuzzling
fluctuation8ofthisvastoceanicriver,whichpoursthroughtheFlorida
&rafts90billlontonsofwarm water everyhour.
J u l y1,1802,after20yearsofmeritorious service i nthegradeoflieutenant,
h e was advancedtothatoflieutenant commander, and August10,1898,to that
ofcommarider.Daringthe Spanish-AmericanWar,he commandedthedyna-
mite cruiserVesuviuaattheslegeofSantiagodeCuba.Thereafterhewasin
chargeoftheequipment departmentattheBostonNavy Yard, andIn1901-2
W a sagain afloat, commandingtheU.5.9.Prairie.Hewas next detailed to
duty in Washingtonasamemberofthe General Board and, assistant to the
Chiefofthe Bureau of Navigation.November10, 1902,hewas promotedto
therankofcaptain.I n1905he became t h e Chief of Staff of the North Atlantic
Fleet, and in1908-9servedasChiefoftheBureau ofNavigation.July4,
1908,h e was madearear admiral, and on December15was retired.In
July,1909,he was relieved from active duty.
Admiral Pillsbury wasafellowoftheAmerican Association fortheAdvance-
mentofSclence,andamemberoftheAmericanAcademyofArtsand
